Fundamental: Cryptographic Primitives

Transaction Malleability

Transaction malleability is a vulnerability that occurs when a transaction on the blockchain is modified in such a way that it changes the transaction ID but still produces the same result. This can happen because the transaction ID is based on the digital signature of the transaction, which can be modified without invalidating the transaction. Transaction malleability can have several effects on the vulnerability of a blockchain.

First, it can lead to issues with the processing of transactions, as the network may not recognize modified transactions. This can result in delays or errors in the processing of transactions and can also create the possibility of double-spending attacks. Second, transaction malleability can make auditing the transactions on the blockchain more difficult. If a transaction is modified, it can be more challenging to track the flow of funds and ensure that all transactions are properly recorded on the blockchain. This can create a risk of fraud or other types of financial malfeasance. Finally, transaction malleability can make it more challenging to develop smart contracts and other applications that rely on the integrity of the transactions on the blockchain. If the transaction ID can be modified, it may be more difficult to develop contracts that can ensure the proper execution of transactions and the system’s security.

Developers must take care to ensure that their systems are designed to address this vulnerability and other potential vulnerabilities in the system. This may involve the development of new protocols and techniques for processing transactions and verifying the integrity of the data on the blockchain.

Protocol: Consensus Mechanisms

51% Attack

A 51% attack, also referred to as a majority attack, is a type of assault on a blockchain network wherein the attacker seizes control of the majority of the network's hash rate or processing power [29]. With such control, the attacker can manipulate the historical records of the blockchain, compromising the overall integrity of the system.

In a blockchain network, transactions are grouped into blocks and sequentially added to the blockchain. Each block includes a cryptographic hash of the preceding one, forming an unbroken chain of blocks. This interconnection makes it exceptionally challenging to tamper with the blockchain, as any modifications to past blocks would be promptly identified by the network.

Nonetheless, an attacker can produce a longer chain of blocks that deviate from the main chain if they possess more processing power than the network. This is referred to as a fork, and if the attacker creates a chain that is longer than the original, it takes over as the network's official chain. This gives the attacker the ability to completely erase all transactions made on the original chain and rewrite the blockchain's history.

Because it erodes user confidence in the network, this could have detrimental effects on the blockchain's integrity. The system as a whole may become unstable, and transactions that were previously regarded as valid may now be regarded as invalid. The attacker might also be able to double-spend coins, which would allow them to spend the same coins multiple times without being detected.

Protocol: Consensus Mechanisms

Long-Range Attack

Validators are chosen to add new blocks and approve transactions in a Proof of Stake (PoS) blockchain according to the quantity of cryptocurrency they own within the network. Because their cryptocurrency holdings are at risk of loss should they conduct dishonestly, these validators are motivated to operate honestly. When an attacker creates a conflicting blockchain version that is longer than the current one and gets it approved by the network, it might be considered a long-range attack on a proof-of-work blockchain. The attack is called ”longrange” because the attacker is trying to rewrite the entire history of the blockchain.

To execute this attack, the attacker would need to acquire a large stake in the network, giving them the ability to create blocks and validate transactions. They would then create a fork of the blockchain starting from a point in the past that is different from the existing blockchain. The attacker would then continue to create new blocks on this fork, ensuring that their fork grows longer than the existing blockchain. The attacker would need to ensure that the nodes in the network switch to their fork and accept it as the legitimate version of the blockchain. This can be achieved through a variety of means, such as performing a 51% attack on the network, bribing or coercing validators to switch to their fork, or launching a Sybil attack to create fake identities and influence the network.

Once the attacker’s fork becomes the accepted version of the blockchain, the integrity of the distributed ledger is compromised. Transactions that were previously considered valid may now be invalid, and the history of the blockchain may have been rewritten to favor the attacker.

To prevent both 51% attacks and long-range attacks, some networks use checkpoints or other mechanisms to prevent forks from occurring, even if an attacker does control the majority of the network’s hash rate.

Protocol: Block Creation

Frontrunning

When a user initiates a transaction on the blockchain, it is initially submitted to the entire network of nodes for validation and approval. After the network verifies the transaction, it is added to the blockchain along with other transactions in a block. The process of mining, which involves nodes competing with one another to solve a cryptographic challenge, adds a block to the blockchain. The first node to solve the puzzle and add the block to the blockchain wins cryptocurrency. This procedure does, however, carry a possible security risk. When an attacker notices a transaction, they can make a rival transaction with a larger transaction fee to execute it before the original one. This is referred to as a “race attack”, which has the ability to nullify or reverse the initial transaction.

Many blockchain prototypes include a technique known as “transaction confirmation” to thwart attacks of this kind. A transaction is originally flagged as unconfirmed when it is broadcast to the network. A node receives one or more confirmations when it is added to a block and validated. A transaction is less likely to be invalidated or overturned if there are more confirmations. Furthermore, “transaction finality” is a mechanism employed by certain blockchain protocols. The transactions included in a block are deemed final once they are uploaded to the blockchain and cannot be removed or changed. This adds another degree of protection and guarantees that a transaction cannot be altered once it is posted to the blockchain.

Infrastructure: Network

Eclipse/Routing Attack

When a hacker manages to take over a user's blockchain network connection, they can essentially isolate them from the rest of the network through an eclipse attack. This gives the attacker the ability to alter the data that the user sends and receives, which they can exploit to either prevent authentic transactions from being added to the blockchain or to create bogus transactions. By rerouting a user's traffic through a fraudulent server or by altering the user's network settings to prohibit them from connecting to the authentic blockchain network, malware can be used to carry out eclipse attacks.

Conversely, routing attacks entail the manipulation of network traffic routing between blockchain network nodes by an attacker. This can be used to stop specific nodes from sending or receiving data or to intercept or alter transactions. Malware can be used to intercept network data on a user's device or change the routing tables of that user in order to carry out routing assaults.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Integer Underflow or Overflow Vulnerability

Integer overflow and underflow vulnerabilities are types of software vulnerabilities that can occur when a program tries to store a value in a variable that is too small to hold. If the value is too large, it can cause an overflow, where the value “wraps around” and starts from the lowest possible value. If the value is too small, it can cause an underflow, where the value becomes negative or starts from the highest possible value.

Blockchain protocols often rely on checks and balances to prevent unauthorized actions, such as transactions that exceed a certain value or attempts to double-spend cryptocurrency. These checks are typically implemented using integers, and if an attacker can exploit an overflow or underflow vulnerability, they may be able to bypass these checks and perform unauthorized actions. For example, an attacker may be able to create a fake transaction that appears to be within the allowed limits but actually causes an overflow or underflow when it is processed. This could allow the attacker to steal cryptocurrency, double-spend funds, or perform other unauthorized actions.

To prevent integer overflow and underflow vulnerabilities, blockchain developers must ensure that all integer values used in their code are properly sized and checked. They must also be aware of the potential for integer vulnerabilities when designing their protocols and implement appropriate checks and balances to prevent unauthorized actions.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Bad Randomness

In general computer science, generating strong randomness is a hard task; in the context of smart contracts, it becomes harder. Self-executing programs known as “smart contracts” run on blockchains and depend on random values to maintain the security and integrity of their operations. For example, to choose the lottery winner, a smart contract managing the lottery might have to come up with a random number. However, creating robust randomness in a predictable, decentralized, and untrustworthy setting such as a blockchain is a difficult undertaking. This is so that all nodes in the network can agree on the current state of the blockchain, which is determined by consensus algorithms used by the blockchain. As a result, any random values produced by smart contracts have to be generated in a fashion that is unpredictable by any one node or set of nodes, and they have to be verifiable by all nodes in the network.

Using external randomness sources, such as external oracles or hardware-based random number generators, is one method of introducing unpredictability into smart contracts. However, because attackers might influence or hack the external sources themselves, this strategy creates new vulnerabilities. An attacker may, for instance, use bribery to force an oracle to provide a biassed random value or initiate a denial-of-service attack against a random number generator that is based on hardware. Using a cryptographic function, like a hash function or signature scheme, to produce a random number depending on the blockchain's current state is another method for adding randomness to smart contracts. However, this approach also has limitations and can be vulnerable to attack. For example, an attacker may be able to manipulate the input to the cryptographic function to bias the resulting random value.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Reentrancy Vulnerability

Reentrancy vulnerability arises when a contract calls another contract without first completing its own execution. This can cause the second contract to execute and potentially call back into the first contract, resulting in an unintended execution path. In more detail, when a contract calls another contract, it temporarily suspends its own execution and passes control to the called contract. However, if the called contract contains a callback function that allows it to call back into the original contract, it may be possible for an attacker to exploit this to cause reentrancy. The attacker can create a malicious contract that contains a function that calls a vulnerable contract and then immediately calls back into itself. This can cause the vulnerable contract to execute its callback function, potentially allowing the attacker to steal funds or cause other unauthorized actions.

To prevent reentrancy vulnerabilities, smart contract developers must be careful to ensure that contracts do not call other contracts before completing their own execution. They should also use safe coding practices and adhere to established security guidelines. Additionally, smart contracts should be thoroughly audited and tested for security vulnerabilities before deployment.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Short Address Vulnerability

Smart contracts are susceptible to attacks known as short-address vulnerabilities, which can lead to the execution of transactions by vulnerable contracts that exceed their authorized limits. The way some Ethereum clients handle transactions with small addresses gives rise to this kind of vulnerability. An Ethereum address is a 20-byte value that is used to uniquely identify a blockchain account. When a transaction is sent to an address, the client sends the transaction along with the target address and the amount of Ether to be transferred. The target address is typically represented as a hexadecimal string with a length of 40 characters, corresponding to 20 bytes. However, some clients allow transactions to be sent to addresses with shorter string lengths, such as 39 or 38 characters. Short address vulnerabilities occur because some smart contracts only check the first 20 bytes of the target address, assuming that the remaining bytes are zero. This assumption is not always valid, as an attacker can create a short address by omitting trailing zeros, resulting in an address that has a different value when interpreted as a 20-byte value. This can allow an attacker to send a transaction with a short address and a value that exceeds the intended amount, causing the vulnerable contract to perform unauthorized actions.

For example, consider a smart contract that accepts payments and credits the sender’s account with a corresponding amount of tokens. The contract may check the first 20 bytes of the target address to ensure that the payment is sent to a valid address. However, if an attacker sends a transaction with a short address and a value that exceeds the intended amount, the vulnerable contract may credit the attacker’s account with more tokens than intended.

To prevent short-address vulnerabilities, smart contract developers should validate the length of input addresses properly and ensure that their contracts are not vulnerable to this type of attack.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Dependence on Timestamps

Block builders with malicious intent may target smart contracts that are programmed to execute at a specified time or before. Because time on the blockchain is not fixed and can be altered by block producers, this vulnerability exists. In essence, smart contracts have the ability to be programmed to perform specific tasks or release money dependent on the time of day. On the other hand, the blockchain's current time is dynamic and determined by the people who add new blocks to the network. This means that a malicious block creator can manipulate the current time to force unanticipated behavior in smart contracts.

For example, consider a smart contract that releases funds to a user after a certain date. If a malicious block creator sets the timestamp on a block to a date before the release date, the contract may mistakenly release the funds prematurely. On the other hand, if the malicious block creator sets the timestamp on a block to a date after the release date, the contract may fail to release the funds at all. This vulnerability has been exploited in several attacks on the Ethereum blockchain, including the 2016 Parity multisig wallet hack and the 2018 delayed Proof-of- Work hard fork attack.

To prevent attacks on time-based smart contracts, smart contract developers should take steps to ensure that their contracts are not vulnerable to manipulation by malicious block creators. One approach is to use trusted time sources, such as the NIST time service or an oracle service, to ensure that the contract is using the correct time. Another approach is to design contracts that do not rely on the current time but instead use block numbers or other deterministic factors to determine when to take action.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Unchecked Call Return Value

n Solidity, some low-level functions throw an exception when something goes wrong, while others return a boolean value of false to indicate failure. For example, the transfer() function in the ERC20 token standard throws an exception if the transfer fails, while the approve() function returns false if the approval fails. When a function throws an exception, the program execution stops, and the current transaction is reverted. This means that any changes made to the state of the contract during the current transaction are rolled back, and any gas used during the transaction is refunded to the caller.

On the other hand, when a function returns false upon failure, the program execution continues as usual. This means that any changes made to the state of the contract during the current transaction remain in effect, and any gas used during the transaction is not refunded. Failing to check return values can lead to unexpected behavior in a smart contract. If a function returns false but the contract execution continues, the contract’s state may be left in an inconsistent or insecure state. This can result in loss of funds, denial of service attacks, or other security issues.

Therefore, it is essential for smart contract developers to carefully consider the behavior of the functions they are using and to check return values appropriately. It is also important to use error-handling mechanisms such as require() or assert() to ensure that unexpected failures are caught and handled properly, as represented in Table 3.

Repudiation (R)

Because all data on a blockchain network is completely transparent, repudiation is typically not jeopardized. In the event that an attacker manages to obtain entire network access through a 51% attack or a long-range attack, the user has the ability to alter block data in order to prevent any data from being transmitted or received.

Information Disclosure (I)

The confidentiality of the data is typically violated when these assaults are carried out. A number of attack vectors, including the previously stated compromised private key, Shor's algorithm, malware assault on nodes, and poor network design, can be used to compromise network secrecy.

Denial of Service (D)

Protocol: Consensus Mechanism

Sudden Withdrawal of Stake

A substantial number of validators who were previously involved in creating and validating blocks stop doing so if an attacker abruptly withdraws a sizable portion of the PoS (Proof-of-Stake) network's mining resources. This lowers the network's total computing power or staked amount. Because the block difficulty goal was first adjusted to take into account the larger number of validators, it is consequently set excessively high for the remaining nodes. Because of the excessively high level of difficulty needed to construct a new block, the remaining validators will have a considerably harder time creating new blocks within the intended block rate.

A Denial-of-Service (DoS) attack could result from this circumstance since the surviving validators are essentially unable to produce new blocks at the required rate. Since the creation of new blocks is a crucial component of every blockchain network, this could seriously impair the security and integrity of the network and seriously interrupt its regular operations.

Protocol: Block Creation

Transaction Flooding

Nodes have to work out a challenging mathematical riddle in order to add a block to the blockchain. The blockchain network may experience delays in the insertion of new blocks to the ledger if an attacker overwhelms it with a high volume of spam transactions. Due to the restricted size and processing capability of each block, a high volume of spam transactions might impede the execution of legal transactions, resulting in congestion and delayed transaction times. Furthermore, any spam transactions that are recorded in the ledger are kept there indefinitely, using up nodes' processing and storage capacity. This may lead to higher node processing and storage expenses, increasing the cost of blockchain network maintenance.

Infrastructure: Nodes

Outdated Blockchain Software

A hard fork occurs when a blockchain protocol undergoes a significant modification that renders it incompatible with the current blockchain. A hard fork divides the blockchain into two distinct chains, each with its own set of features and regulations. After a hard fork, users need to update their software to the most recent version that supports the new chain in order to continue utilizing the blockchain. If the software isn not updated, a user can be stuck on the previous chain and unable to enjoy the current chain's features.

Let's take a scenario where a hard fork occurs in a blockchain network, and a new consensus algorithm is implemented. Then, nodes that do notupgrade their software to accommodate the new algorithm will be unable to mine new blocks for the new chain or validate transactions. Consequently, when users switch to the new chain, they will be left behind on the old chain, which can become outdated.

Infrastructure: Network

Physical Attacks

The decentralized nature of blockchain solutions provides a high degree of security against attacks, as there is no central point of failure. However, physical attacks on the communication links or devices that make up the blockchain network can still pose a significant threat to the system’s functionality. An attacker who physically severs communication links or tampers with devices, such as routers, switches, or servers, could cause the blockchain solution’s functionality to be degraded. When communication links are cut off, nodes on the blockchain network may not be able to communicate with each other, leading to the system’s fragmentation. This could prevent transactions from being validated and added to the blockchain, leading to a loss of trust in the system.

In addition, an attacker could compromise the integrity of the blockchain network by tampering with devices such as routers. By intercepting and manipulating network traffic, an attacker could potentially redirect transactions to their own nodes or insert fraudulent transactions into the blockchain. This could compromise the system’s security and undermine the trust of users in the network.

Infrastructure: Network

PoS DoS

In a PoS blockchain, block creators are selected based on their stake in the network, which means that the more tokens a user holds, the more likely they are to be selected to create the next block. A DoS attack against the legitimate block creator involves overwhelming their computer system or network with a large number of requests or traffic, making it difficult or impossible for them to validate transactions and create a block. As a result of the attack, the legitimate block creator may miss their opportunity to create a block, which can cause a delay in the processing of transactions and a decrease in the overall efficiency and capacity of the blockchain. This can have a ripple effect on the entire network, as the delay in block creation can cause other users to miss their opportunity to validate transactions and earn rewards for doing so.

Furthermore, a DoS attack against the legitimate block creator can also lead to the centralization of the blockchain network. If the attack is successful in preventing the legitimate block creator from creating a block, the network may turn to a centralized solution, such as a single entity or a smaller group of users, to create blocks, which can undermine the decentralization and security of the blockchain.

A PoS blockchain should have robust security features like firewalls, intrusion detection systems, and rate-limiting mechanisms installed to lessen the likelihood of a DoS assault on the authorized block maker. Additionally, it is essential to maintain a diverse group of block makers in order to prevent network centralization and ensure the security and stability of the blockchain.

Infrastructure: Network

Membership Services Provider (MSP) DoS

In a blockchain system that uses a permissioned model, membership services providers (MSPs) are responsible for managing the access and permissions of users to the network. A Denial of Service (DoS) attack against an MSP can have serious consequences, as it can deny legitimate users access to the blockchain system. A DoS attack against an MSP can take many forms, including flooding the system with a large volume of traffic, overwhelming the system with requests, or exploiting vulnerabilities in the MSP’s software or hardware. The objective of a DoS attack is to cause the MSP to become unavailable or unresponsive, which can prevent legitimate users from accessing the blockchain system. The consequences of a DoS attack on an MSP can be severe, as it can prevent users from validating transactions, creating new blocks, and participating in the consensus process. This may result in a drop in system efficiency, a delay in transaction processing, and a decline in user confidence in the blockchain network. It is crucial to put strong security measures in place, including firewalls, intrusion detection systems, and rate-limiting techniques, to reduce the chance of a DoS assault on an MSP. Maintaining a diversified range of MSPs is also crucial to avoiding single points of failure and guaranteeing the system's availability and dependability.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Failure to Control Access

Protected kill switches are a feature of smart contracts that enable specific people or organizations to end or alter the contract in the event of faults or unanticipated circumstances. A Denial of Service (DoS) attack against the smart contract could be possible, though, if access to these features is not restricted. If an attacker manages to obtain unauthorized access to the function that governs the switch, they may launch a denial-of-service attack against a smart contract that has a protected kill switch. For instance, an attacker can terminate the contract or change its terms if they manage to obtain the private key of the person or organization in charge of the kill switch. This may have a number of detrimental effects, including nullifying or changing the terms of the contract, jeopardizing its security, or delaying the completion of transactions. Moreover, a denial-of-service assault targeting a smart contract equipped with a protected kill switch has the potential to erode the legitimacy and confidence in the blockchain system. A loss of trust in the network may result from the shutdown or alteration of a significant or extensively utilized contract, which would lower the value of the coin linked to it.

A smart contract with a protected kill switch is less vulnerable to a DoS attack when robust access control measures are implemented. This may entail using multi-factor authentication, role-based access controls, and ongoing monitoring to make sure that only authorized individuals or entities have access to the function in charge of maintaining the switch. Additionally, to guarantee the availability and integrity of the smart contract and the related data, it is essential to put in place the proper security measures, such as encryption and backup and recovery protocols.

Advanced: Smart Contracts

Shortage of Gas

The usage of gas, a unit of measurement that establishes the cost of carrying out transactions and smart contracts on the network, is one of Ethereum's distinctive features. The amount of gas needed to complete each operation in a smart contract varies, and the total amount of gas used in a transaction or contract establishes its cost. Ethereum caps the amount of gas that a transaction or contract can utilize in order to stop abuse and guarantee the stability and dependability of the network. A smart contract can be used to launch a denial of service (DoS) assault if it is driven into a situation where it requires more gas than it has allocated to operate.

A DoS attack that exploits the gas limit can occur if an attacker intentionally creates a smart contract that requires an excessive amount of gas to execute. The attacker can then broadcast a transaction that attempts to execute the contract with the maximum amount of gas allowed by the network. This will cause the transaction to fail, consuming all the gas allocated to it and preventing other transactions and contracts from being executed on the network. In addition to the financial cost of executing the transaction, the attack can cause significant delays in the processing of other transactions and contracts, leading to a decrease in the overall efficiency of the network. Moreover, a sustained DoS attack can also undermine the trust and credibility of the Ethereum network, leading to a decrease in the value of the cryptocurrency associated with it.

It is crucial to make sure that the smart contracts created on the Ethereum network are efficient and optimized in order to reduce the possibility of a denial-of-service attack that takes advantage of the gas restriction. This may entail lowering the quantity of gas used for each operation and cutting down on the number of operations necessary to complete the contract. Furthermore, it is critical to put in place suitable security measures, such as ongoing surveillance and rate-limiting techniques, to stop excessive gas use from overloading the network, as represented in Table 4.

Table 4 Denial of service threats.
Protocol Level Consensus Mechanism Sudden Withdrawal of stake
Block Creation Transaction Flooding
Infrastructure Level Nodes Outdated Blockchain Software

Elevated Privileges (E)

Protocol: Consensus Mechanism

Selfish Mining

Malicious miners in blockchain networks employ the technique known as “selfish mining” in an effort to outwit other miners and take more control over the distributed ledger. The way the attack operates is that it gives the attacker more control over the distribution and validation of transactions on the network by enabling them to create more blocks than their share of mining power should. The attack's foundation is the notion that miners in a blockchain network compete to produce blocks and that the first person to figure out each block's cryptographic puzzle wins a set quantity of bitcoin. When someone engages in a selfish mining assault, they try to conceal the blocks they have already mined from other users of the network, postponing their broadcast until they have mined more blocks. By doing this, the attacker can split the blockchain privately and continue mining new blocks without the other users of the network knowing about it.

The attacker can broadcast their private fork of the blockchain, forcing the network to convert to their version of the ledger once they have mined more blocks than their proportion of mining power should permit. This essentially renders the efforts of other miners operating on the authentic blockchain illegitimate, endowing the attacker with further control over the verification and dissemination of transactions throughout the network. A selfish mining attack can have devastating effects since it gives the attacker control over a large chunk of the network and the ability to sway transactions to their advantage. By generating and broadcasting two contradictory transactions, for instance, an attacker can double-spend bitcoin, causing the network to lose trust and the value of the coin that is connected with it to drop.

Blockchain networks frequently include features to discourage dishonest behavior and stop miners from postponing the broadcast of their blocks in order to reduce the possibility of selfish mining assaults. Certain networks, for instance, penalize miners who try to postpone the broadcast of their blocks, while other networks employ consensus algorithms that compensate miners for adhering to the network's rules and penalize those that try to cheat, as depicted in Table 5.

Table 5 Elevated privileges threats.
Protocol Level Consensus Mechanism Selfish Mining
Infrastructure Level Nodes MSP Misconfig

Infrastructure: Nodes

MSP Misconfg

MSPs are essential to a permissioned blockchain network because they guarantee that only individuals with permission may access the network and carry out certain tasks, such as adding new blocks or submitting transactions. An attacker may be able to give them unauthorized blockchain permissions if an MSP is configured incorrectly. For instance, if an MSP is configured to award authorizations in accordance with a participant's public key, an adversary can fabricate a public key and utilize it to obtain entry into the network. In a similar vein, an attacker may pose as a legitimate participant in order to obtain access if an MSP is configured to grant rights based on a participant's position or organizational affiliation.

An attacker may engage in a variety of destructive behaviors, including stealing bitcoin, altering transactions, or interfering with the network's regular operation if they have gained unauthorized access to the system.

Blockchain networks usually use multi-factor authentication and tight access constraints to confirm participant identities in order to reduce the possibility of incorrectly setup MSPs. Furthermore, routine audits of MSP installations can guarantee that access restrictions are operating as intended and that they are configured correctly.

FUTURE WORK

To strengthen the threat analysis performed in this paper on blockchain-based supply chain management systems, researchers may perform threat analysis using various other frameworks such as LINDDUN, DREAD, PASTA, VAST, etc.

CONCLUSION

In this paper, we have categorized the blockchain ecosystem into 4 levels: fundamental, protocol, infrastructure, and advanced levels. Each of these levels consists of several components that all make up the blockchain. Threats were identified in each of these levels and then categorized into Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information Disclosure, Denial of Service, and Elevated Privilege (STRIDE) categories. This is to account for the difference in access control and access points in the dataflow diagram of each level.
