Revolutionizing Hen Care in Smart Poultry Farming: The Impact of AI-Driven Sensors on Optimizing Avian Health



P. Deepan1, *, R. Vidya1, N. Arul1, S. Dhiravidaselvi2, Shashi Kant Gupta3
1 Department of CSM, St. Peter’s Engineering College, Hyderabad-500043, India
2 Department of CSE, Roever Engineering College, Tamil Nadu-621212, India
3 Computer Science and Engineering, Eudoxia Research University, New Castle, USA

Abstract

Automation is taking over the entire globe. To boost efficiency, businesses, governments, and nonprofits are all using automation in their own fields. The demand for and necessity of automation is high in the agricultural industry. The majority of nations have begun to supply and use smart farming solutions. This proposed study focuses on smart poultry farming, an essential aspect of farming, and designs a new prototype. A number of Indian farmers keep chickens as pets, yet their farms' output, longevity, efficiency, and treatment of animals are all lacking.

Therefore, sustainable and lucrative farming is the outcome of integrating cutting-edge technology such as robotics, Internet of Things (IoT) sensors, and artificial intelligence (AI). This prototype incorporates a number of Internet of Things (IoT) sensors for several features, such as lighting (LDR), air quality (MG135), water quality (pH), temperature (DTH11), and lighting (LDR). Minimizing human intervention, keeping tabs on the bird's well-being, making the most of available resources, and increasing output are all outcomes of the automated system's development. With the help of an AI-IoT smart system, poultry producers will be able to manage their farms more efficiently and triumph over a number of obstacles.

Keywords: Agricultural, Automation, Artificial intelligence, Hen health, Internet of Things, Poultry farming, Smart farming, Sensors.

* Corresponding author P. Deepan: Department of CSM, St. Peter’s Engineering College, Hyderabad-500043, India; E-mail: deepanp87@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

Agriculture is the backbone of the Indian economy. Many Indians rely on agriculture as their primary source of income. Integrating smart technologies into

traditional farming methods enhances overall productivity and increases the income of the farmers [1-5]. Poultry farming is a broader idea behind smart agriculture. It focuses on raising chickens for meat production and egg production. New techniques and technologies are added to every stage of traditional poultry farming practice to overcome traditional farming limitations and optimize productivity, efficiently utilize resources, and focus on the welfare of animals [6-9].

Smart poultry farms consist of different sensors, tools, and techniques to build an optimized and automated farming technology for farm management. The main goal behind such smart farms is to enhance efficiency and productivity. There are several factors that need to be considered in designing a prototype [10, 11]. They include an environment that is automatically monitored and controlled using sensors and IoT devices. The environmental attributes taken into consideration are temperature, humidity, ventilation, water quality, and lighting [12-14]. The physical attributes recorded are the chicken's health and feed. The chickens that are kept only under an optimal setup will yield enhanced productivity. The observations are monitored and stored at repeated intervals of time. This data can be further used to extract insights into poultry farms and derive trends and patterns for further decision-making processes. The health of the chicken is interrelated to all other parameters [15-17]. An automated system to operate and control the poultry farm is designed to concentrate on the food requirement, monitor health and nutritional information, and check on the water quality and continuous supply of water [18-21]. The focus is on checking the temperature, humidity, and lighting of the environmental setup of the poultry farm. An automated egg collection system to reduce manpower can be designed to collect the eggs from the poultry farm, segregate the eggs based on their quality, and dispatch them for sale [22]. The poultry farm can be monitored and controlled from a remote location. Real-time updates and alert messages are sent through mobiles to the corresponding person of the poultry farm. The overall farm management can be improved by integrating automation techniques, streamlining farm operations, optimizing the utilization of resources, remote monitoring, and enhancing animal care [23-26].

Several factors have contributed to the requirement for this kind of smart poultry farming system, including the rising demand for poultry products like meat and eggs, the efficient use of energy, the constant monitoring of animal health, the difficulties and issues posed by a lack of labor, and the need for early disease detection to prevent financial losses [27]. There are several challenges in designing and successfully setting up a poultry farm for Indian farmers. Very detailed and broader background research on proper planning on the infrastructure size and capacity, area and environment to set up the poultry farm, automation techniques to be implemented, sensors to be used, and health and welfare monitoring techniques are required [28].

Smart poultry farms will provide a promising livelihood for Indian farmers. It will lead to the development and sustainable growth of the agricultural sector and enhance the overall Indian economy. The Indian farmers should learn, adopt, and implement smart techniques in the poultry farms for a successful outcome.

Limitations in Traditional Poultry Farming

Traditional poultry farming has the following problems:

  • Traditional poultry farming requires more manpower to maintain the farms.
  • Difficult to monitor the climate conditions earlier.
  • Earlier bird disease prediction and identification are very difficult in traditional poultry farming.

The integration of smart poultry automated systems can be made possible through APIs to manage different software activities.

Background of the Invention

Agriculture is the backbone of the Indian economy. Many Indians rely on agriculture as their primary source of income. Integrating smart technologies into traditional farming methods enhances overall productivity and increases the income of the farmers. Poultry farming is a broader idea behind smart agriculture. It focuses on raising chickens for meat production and egg production. New techniques and technologies are added to every stage of traditional poultry farming practice to overcome traditional farming limitations and optimize productivity, efficiently utilize resources, and focus on the welfare of animals.

The need for this kind of smart poultry farming system is triggered by several factors like increased demand for poultry products like meat and eggs, proper utilization of energy resources, continuous monitoring of animal health, challenges and issues in facing a labor shortage, and early disease detection to prevent financial losses.

Challenges in Poultry Farming

Environmental factors, dust, and fluctuation can affect the accuracy of the sensors; therefore, we need to maintain the sensors to ensure reliability.

Summary of the Research

This chapter can be broken down into the following sections: In the second section, we will discuss and compare the various studies carried out by the researchers. In the third section, the hardware and software requirements of an intelligent smart poultry farming monitoring system are described. In the fourth section, we will discuss the procedure that was followed when developing the proposed smart poultry farming monitoring system, as well as the experiments carried out and the findings of the system discussed. The final section gives a conclusion and discusses the work that will be done in the future.

LITERATURE REVIEW

In order to monitor and regulate environmental conditions on a chicken farm using smart farming systems, this part focuses on the contributions that have been made and the solutions that have been recommended with a smart poultry farming system. For instance, the authors Poolsanguan S. et al. [29] have focused on a combined strategy of the sensor network and mobile devices in order to regulate and monitor the environmental conditions that are present in the chicken farm as well as the constraints that are imposed on them. They make it possible for the owner to monitor a range of environmental data by sending a text message to the owner's mobile device. This allows the owner to keep track of things like a change in temperature or the amount of humidity in the air. It is possible for the system to respond to the user by sending a command by message to the control system to either increase or decrease the speed of the fan in order to bring the environment of the birds up to a comfortable level. This would be done in order to bring the birds' environment up to a comfortable level. It was decided to put into action the intelligent poultry farm monitoring system in order to exercise some degree of control over the farm at a cost that was not overly burdensome.

Mohammad Ibrahim and his colleagues [30] offer a way to develop a cost-effective and standardized environmental monitoring system that uses the Raspberry Pi (R-Pi) single-board computer. Python was the chosen programming language for the construction of the system and an Internet of Things platform was utilized so that the system could be accessed and managed from a remote location. It does this by utilizing sensors to gather information about the surrounding environment, which is then immediately uploaded to the internet, where it can be accessed whenever and wherever there is a connection to the internet. This information can be accessed whenever necessary and in any location. The findings of the studies demonstrated that the system is capable of performing accurate measurements of a variety of environmental factors, including temperature, humidity, light level, and quantities of the potentially lethal contaminant carbon monoxide. These measurements were carried out with the intention of achieving the target of having lower levels of carbon monoxide emissions. It accomplishes this by first collecting data from sensors positioned in its immediate surroundings and then directly uploading that data to the internet.

Fangwu and colleagues have suggested [31] that an online monitoring system for chicken farms may be constructed using the ZigBee module as its foundation. It will provide a network that is capable of real-time monitoring, and this monitoring system will consist of TI's CC2430 control nodes, which are based on ZigBee interface technology, as well as node controllers, data receivers, and data transmission. Additionally, it will provide a network that is able to deliver data in real time. CO2 sensors employ the TGS4161 sensor to measure various environmental variables, whereas temperature and humidity sensors use the SHT75 sensor. Both types of sensors monitor the environment. By first doing an analysis of the data transmission of the system and then working to reduce the ZigBee protocol stack, the purpose of this project was to develop the protocols for data transmission and the communication formats that the system would use.

According to the findings of Ayyappan V. et al. [32], a poultry farm's temperature, humidity, and ammonia gas levels are continuously monitored and automatically modified in order to achieve the highest possible level of chicken production. Additionally, the sensor module can be utilized to control and monitor the level of the water. Connecting all of the sensor modules to the microcontroller is how all of the sensor values are collected; after that, the Wi-Fi module is responsible for uploading the data to the website. As long as the poultry farm in question has internet connectivity, the farm's management can use a mobile device, such as a laptop or smartphone, to monitor the conditions inside the building. This system will automatically manage the temperature, humidity, and ammonia gas and water levels. It will accomplish this by utilizing a ventilation window, an exhaust fan, a cooling fan, and a DC motor.

Rupali, B. and S. S. Sonavane [33] have focused their research on the control and automatic monitoring of environmental variables within a chicken farm through the integration of wireless sensors and a GPRS network. The individual in charge of the poultry farm who implements this tactic will be able to get a text message sent to the cellphone number that they have on file, alerting them of the present state of the internal environment of the farm. The system will immediately put into motion the necessary processes to take control of the relevant environmental parameters whenever there is an unexpected change in the weather. The ATMEGA324A microcontroller, which is connected to all of the sensors, is able to manage and monitor all of the data because it is connected to all of the sensors. Because of this, the mechanism that regulates the water level and the mechanism that regulates the food supply may both be monitored and managed with the help of sensors. The implementation of this technology results in the establishment of a monitoring system with the objective of guaranteeing that healthy conditions are maintained in poultry farms, even in the absence of human interaction.

The above-mentioned literature is mostly related to either the Internet of Things (IoT) or wireless sensor networks. The works are not saturated in terms of efficient monitoring of modern smart poultry farming. So, in order to tackle this issue, we have to develop an intelligent AI and IoT-based smart poultry farming monitoring system. The IoT part concentrates on collecting climate or environmental data through sensors. Then, the AI module reacts based on the sensor data information.

Objectives of the Research Work

The main intention of the research is to develop an automated smart poultry farming system using intelligent AI and IoT technologies to increase productivity and quality to improve the livelihood of poor farmers in villages.

DESIGN THE PROTOTYPE

Within this section, the hardware design component and software requirements of the smart poultry farming monitoring system are broken down and discussed. To record environmental conditions in poultry farming, a smart poultry farm is proposed in Fig. (1) by integrating a number of Internet of Things (IoT) sensors such as temperature and humidity sensors (DTH11), lighting sensors (LDR), air quality sensors (MG135), cameras, thermal camera sensors, water quality sensors (pH), and GSM modules.

Fig. (1))
Smart Poultry Farm Arduino Microcontroller.

Arduino UNO Board

Arduino is an open-source board that may be used to program microcontrollers. It is composed of user-friendly software and hardware components. In order to complete this project, an Arduino UNO was utilized. The Arduino UNO is a board that can be used as a microcontroller. It has a clock speed of 10 MHz, an operating voltage of 5 V, and ATMEGA328P controller chips as its main component. The ATMEGA328P on the Arduino Uno is a board that comes pre-programmed and has a bootloader. This board eliminates the need for an external hardware programmer, making it possible to upload fresh code at any time. The ATMEGA328P Arduino UNO is utilized as the single-chip microcontroller for this project. Additionally, it consumes a minuscule amount of gasoline. The inputs may be read on Arduino boards, and the readings can then be converted into output. The sample arduino board for healthcare system is shown in Fig. (2).

Fig. (2))
Arduino UNO Board Microcontroller.

DTH11 Sensor

The temperature in the environment will change a lot, often reaching dangerously high levels in the summer and then dropping to dangerously low levels in the winter. Both of these things have a direct effect on the well-being of animals and the health of poultry chickens, causing diseases like Bird Flu, hand and foot diseases, and many others. This device uses the DHT11 temperature monitor to measure the air's temperature and humidity. Both Fahrenheit and Celsius can be used to measure the temperature of this sensor. This sensor costs very little and can record both temperature and humidity. There is a capacitive humidity sensor. It also uses a thermistor to figure out how clean the air is around it. The output or unit of measurement is found online. The sample DTH11 sensor is described in Fig. (3).

Fig. (3))
DTH11 Sensor Module.

MQ135 Sensor

The MQ-135 is a simple yet effective method for determining the quality of the air. It has the ability to detect the presence of a wide variety of gases. Gas leaks can be detected in both residential and commercial settings with its help. It is able to detect smoke, i-butane, LPG, propane, methane, alcohol, and hydrogen in addition to these other gases. The MQ-135 was chosen because of its quick reaction, its ability to adjust its sensitivity, and its use as a gas leakage detector for the Grove connector. The sample MQ135 sensor is shown in Fig. (4).

Fig. (4))
MQ135 Sensor Module.

LDR Sensor

A light-dependent resistor, also known as an LDR, can also be referred to as a photoresistor, a photoconductor, or a cadmium sulfide (CdS) cell. These are just a few of the many names for this type of component. Photoconductivity is the term used to describe the core concept that underlies how it works. The passive component takes the shape of a resistor in its most fundamental state. The value of the resistor's resistance decreases as the amount of light that enters the system increases. This sensor is used to detect the amount of light that falls inside the chamber, and based on this amount, it is possible to determine whether or not the light bulb is currently in the ON or OFF position. For its healthy development, it is absolutely necessary for there to be an adequate quantity of light. The LDR sensor module is described in Fig. (5).

Fig. (5))
LDR Sensor Module.

LCD Display

LCD stands for “liquid crystal display”, which refers to a sort of display that relies primarily on the usage of liquid crystals as its primary form of operation. A flat panel display is one example of this type of display. LEDs can be found in a wide range of consumer and commercial items, including instrument panels, televisions, cell phones, and computer monitors, to name a few. The panel's primary use is to utilize a conventional overhead projector in order to display the on-screen content of a microcomputer on a display that is far larger than the original. Because of this, it is possible for big audiences to get the information without needing to congregate around the TV display. The LCD sensor module is described in Fig. (6).

Fig. (6))
LCD Sensor Module.

GSM Module

An Arduino board can be used in conjunction with a GSM module to provide internet connectivity, send and receive SMS messages, and create phone calls by utilizing the GSM library. The module functions as a GSM modem and may be linked to a PCB. The PCB generates a range of signals, including TTL output, and the module can communicate with it. A GSM system that is outfitted with a GSM module serves two basic functions: the first is to determine whether or not an unauthorized person has entered controlled territory, and the second is to notify the owner of the property by phoning or texting the owner's phone in the event that an unauthorized person has entered controlled area. A GSM module has been installed in the control panel, and the protected area has been supplied with all of the necessary sensors. This is the fundamental guiding idea behind how a GSM alarm system functions. The GSM module sensor is described in Fig. (7).

Fig. (7))
GSM Sensor Module.

Thermal Sensor

Sensors that track temperature fluctuations are called thermal sensors. Temperature sensors are utilized in a wide variety of process-based businesses to measure the thermal properties of gases, liquids, and solids. Temperature sensors are designed for both general and specialized applications. The use of thermal imaging makes it possible to identify abnormally high temperatures, such as those that occur in the case of a fever, as well as the presence of probable illnesses. Additionally, it is able to detect uncomfortable inflammation in a patient who is either under anesthesia or undergoing surgery. The sample camera sensor module is illustrated in Fig. (8).

Fig. (8))
Thermal Camera Sensor Module.

COLLECTING THE DATA AND MONITORING THE HEN HEALTH

Raspberry Pi is used for data collection, data processing, and data communication and acts as the central processing unit (CPU). Arduino Pi is used for interfacing with sensors. A hybrid network topology comprising a start around Arduino and Mesh for Raspberry configuration is used. Message Queuing Telemetry Transport (MQTT) is a lightweight protocol used, and Hyper Text Transfer (HTTP) is used for transferring data between the cloud servers for data storage and analysis.

With the assistance of artificial intelligence (AI) and the Internet of Things (IoT) technology, we will provide a description of the smart poultry farming model that we have presented in the next subsection. Fig. (9) depicts the whole operation of the proposed smart farm, which is based on AI and the Internet of Things.

Fig. (9))
Workflow Block Diagram of Smart Poultry Farming.

INTEGRATION OF IOT SENSORS IN THE ARCHITECTURE

Temperature (DTH11) sensor, water, quality (pH) sensor, humidity sensor, light (LDR) sensor, and air quality (MG135) sensor are the sensors installed in this poultry farm prototype, and these sensors have ports connecting to the central system by implementing the interfacing techniques like interfacing I2C sensors, interfacing SPI sensors, and interfacing UART sensors. The communication protocol used is I2C (Inter-Integrated Circuit), which is employed in data transportation from one microcontroller to sensors like temperature and humidity sensors. SPI (Serial Peripheral Interface) is particularly useful when there is a need to send the signal in a limited amount of time with sensors such as thermal cameras. UART (Universal Asynchronous Receiver/Transmitter) is used in the GSM mode, as well as in all the other devices that are functioning based on a serial interface.

The data is processed using the microcontroller Arduino UNO and Raspberry Pi. The SM modules are used to transmit the data to the cloud, and machine learning and analytics techniques are used to extract insights, draw patterns, and promote decision-making. The sensor information is used to control the environment, monitor the health of the hen, and effectively utilize the resources.

In Fig. (9), the variable pH represents an analysis of the quality of the water, Te represents an analysis of the temperature, Hu represents an analysis of the humidity, Li represents an analysis of the intensity of the light, and Ng represents an analysis of the quality of the air.

Artificial intelligence and the Internet of Things-based smart chicken farm that has been proposed monitors the poultry farm itself, including the temperature, humidity, air quality, and light levels. Also, the accessibility of food is monitored in real time through the use of a camera sensor at predetermined time intervals. The Light-Density-Response (LDR) sensor is utilized to measure the amount of light that comes from the smart farming system. If the light intensity (Li) is lower than 10M, the LDR sensor will activate the lights automatically. According to Fig. (10a), intelligent poultry farming can be achieved with lighting conditions that are either normal or sufficient. Fig. (10b) shows that the required level of intensity for automated farming systems has not been met by smart poultry farming; hence, the ON state of the light indicator is not enabled.

Fig. (10))
(a) Smart poultry farming with sufficient light intensity and (b) without sufficient light intensity.

In order to get information on the temperature of the farming environment, the temperature sensor (DTH11) is employed. If the temperature of the farm is higher than 32 degrees Celsius, the DTH11 sensor will activate the Cooler FAN. If the temperature of the farm is lower than 32 degrees Celsius, the system will remain in the idle stage (the OFF stage in the FAN), and it will continue to monitor the temperature at regular intervals. As can be seen in Fig. (11a), the intelligent poultry farming system must have a temperature that is higher than the threshold value in order to activate the FAN, which is responsible for preserving both the temperature and the environment's consistency. According to what is shown in Fig. (11b), the system will have typical temperatures as well as humidity levels. In a similar fashion, the humidity is noted by the humidity sensor. If there is an increase in humidity of more than 65%, the sensor will turn on the heater. The work process of smart poultry farming with temperature, humidity and without temperature, humidity is illustrated in Fig. (11).

Fig. (11))
Smart Poultry Farming (a) with Temperature, Humidity and (b) without Temperature, Humidity.

The air quality sensor, also known as the MQ135 sensor, is utilized in the process of poultry farming in order to monitor air quality. If there is a higher concentration of nitrogen gas in the air, the sensor will ring an alarm for the user, and it will also activate the exhaust fan. The automated smart poultry farming has a level of nitrogen gas that is higher than the threshold value, as shown in Fig. (12a); as a result, the system is programmed to automatically enable the exhaust fan and to signal the authorized farmers with the relevant information. In a similar vein, Fig. (12b) demonstrates that the intelligent poultry system has a normal gas level (that is, it does not have a nitrogen gas level).

At the same time, a thermal photo sensor is utilized in order to keep a close eye on the hen's behavior. The thermal sensor is able to provide accurate predictions regarding the temperature of the birds. In order to extract characteristics from thermal camera images, artificial intelligence technologies, such as the SIFT feature extraction methodology and SVM classifiers, are utilized. As a result, the sensor is rather beneficial for detecting and forecasting disease at an earlier stage. The GSM module sends an alert message to the registered mobile device as soon as the sensor and the AI tool predict any sickness symptoms. The camera sensor collects data about the many kinds of food that are available on farms. The water sensor, which measures pH, conducts an assessment of the water's overall quality. In intelligent poultry farming, the LDR sensor is utilized to monitor the lighting conditions of the chicken pens. Fig. (13) depicts a sample of the proposed prototype for your viewing pleasure.

Fig. (12))
Smart poultry prototype (a) with gas and air pollution (b) without gas and air pollution.
Fig. (13))
Smart poultry prototype.

The training samples are collected using different sensors, and the SVM classifier uses insights into the temperature, humidity, quality of the air, and health of the bird. The training samples are initially preprocessed, enhancing the quality of the data. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is used to reduce the dimensionality of the data. The SVM model is evaluated using metrics like accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score. The real-time deployment challenges include maintaining the accuracy of the model over time, ensuring low latency for decision-making, and handling the large volume of data.

Optimization Strategies

The data compression technique is used to reduce the volume of data transmitted to the cloud storage, reducing the bandwidth. A power management algorithm for optimization and load balancing techniques is used for managing the loads and maintaining scalability.

Contribution of the Research

The term “smart poultry farming” is used to describe the practice of utilizing cutting-edge technologies and data-driven strategies to enhance many facets of the chicken production process. It involves utilizing technologies like the IoT, AI, big data analytics, and automation to improve the poultry business. We have compiled a short summary of the benefits that have resulted from wise poultry farming:

1. The proposed smart poultry farm automates the overall operations of traditional poultry farming through advanced tools and techniques.

2. It reduces manpower in various levels of poultry farming practice.

3. The proposed system is developed to send alert messages to the corresponding farmers in case of an emergency.

4. Smart poultry farms act as risk-free systems with enhanced sustainability and improved productivity.

5. It fulfills the needs and emerging demands in the poultry farming sector.

CONCLUSION

Smart poultry farming offers significant benefits to Indian farmers by revolutionizing traditional farming practices. It empowers farmers with real-time monitoring and control capabilities, enabling them to create optimal environments for poultry, reduce mortality rates, and improve overall productivity. The integration of advanced technologies (Artificial Intelligence, Internet of Things) and data analytics facilitates proactive disease management, minimizing the spread of infections and reducing the need for antibiotics. Moreover, smart poultry farming optimizes resource utilization, leading to improved sustainability and reduced environmental impact. The availability of data-driven insights enables farmers to make informed decisions and enhance the efficiency and profitability of their operations. Furthermore, the traceability and quality assurance features of smart farming provide consumers with greater transparency and confidence in the poultry products they consume. Smart poultry farming is a transformative approach that combines technology, data, and animal welfare considerations to revolutionize the poultry industry. By adopting smart farming practices, poultry farmers can achieve higher productivity, improved animal welfare, sustainable resource management, and enhanced food safety, ultimately meeting the growing demand for poultry products in a responsible and efficient manner.

In the future, we will have to use robots, and automation in poultry farming is likely to increase. Robots can assist in tasks such as collecting eggs, cleaning the environment, and even monitoring bird health. Automation can streamline processes like feeding, watering, and waste management, reducing labor requirements and improving efficiency. These developments will further enhance productivity, animal welfare, disease management, and environmental sustainability in the poultry industry, leading to more efficient and responsible poultry production. Predictive analytics can be used to develop automated models in the future.

Deep Learning-Powered Visual Augmentation for the Visually Impaired



Gandrapu Satya Sai Surya Subrahmanya Venkata Krishna Mohan1, *, Mahammad Firose Shaik2, G. Usandra Babu3, Manikandan Hariharan4, Kiran Kumar Patro1
1 Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Aditya Institute of Technology and Management, Tekkali, India
2 Department of Electronics and Instrumentation Engineering, Velagapudi Ramakrishna Siddhartha Engineering College, Deemed to be University, Vijayawada, India
3 Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Aditya University, Surampalem, Andhra Pradesh, India
4 CMR Institute of Technology, Bangaluru, India

Abstract

The interdisciplinary convergence of computer vision and object detection is pivotal for advancing intelligent image analysis. This research surpasses conventional object recognition methodologies by delving into a more nuanced understanding of images, akin to human visual comprehension. It explores deep learning and established object detection systems such as convolutional neural networks (CNN), Region-based CNN (R-CNN), and you only look once (YOLO). The proposed model excels in real-time object recognition, outperforming its predecessors, as previous systems typically detect only a limited number of objects in an image and are most effective at a distance of 5-6 meters. Uniquely, it employs Google Translate for the verbal identification of detected objects, offering a crucial accessibility feature for individuals with visual impairments. This study integrates computer vision, deep learning, and real-time object recognition to enhance visual perception, providing valuable assistance to those facing visual challenges. The proposed method utilizes the Common Objects in Context (COCO) dataset for image comprehension, employing object detection and object tracking with a deep neural network (DNN). The system's output is converted into spoken words through a text-to-speech feature, empowering visually impaired individuals to comprehend their surroundings effectively. The implementation involves key technologies such as NumPy, OpenCV, pyttsx3, PyWin32, OpenCV-contrib- python, and winsound, contributing to a comprehensive system for computer vision and audio processing. Results demonstrate successful execution, with the camera consistently detecting and labeling 5-6 objects in real time.

Keywords: Accessibility technology, Assistive technology, Artificial Intelligence, Computer Vision, COCO data Set, Deep learning, Deep object analysis, DNN, Google Translator, Human-like object understanding, Image understanding, Inclusive design, IoT, LiDAR, Multimodal object interaction, NumPy, Object detection, Real-time object recognition, Text to speech, Visual impairment assistance.

* Corresponding author Gandrapu Satya Sai Surya Subrahmanya Venkata Krishna Mohan: Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Aditya Institute of Technology and Management, Tekkali, India; E-mail: gandrapu.krishnamohan@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

As artificial intelligence (AI) and the Internet of Things (IoT) advance rapidly, numerous smart devices can link with one another [1]. Various innovative concepts, such as 'Smarter Planet', 'Smart City', 'Smart Community', and 'Smart Campus,' have emerged and integrated into various aspects of human life.

The human eye plays a vital role in the visual system, but it is also susceptible to external factors, including potential exposure to pathogens. The eyes can serve as an entry point for certain viruses to infiltrate the body, leading to possible eye diseases and vision impairment [2]. The World Health Organization (WHO) estimated that by 2019, more than 220 million people worldwide would experience visual impairment, presenting substantial daily challenges [3]. While the traditional white cane remains a widely used tool for the visually impaired [4], it has notable limitations and safety concerns. Therefore, the objective of this work is explicit and compelling: to develop a cost-effective and highly efficient solution that empowers visually impaired individuals to navigate their surroundings with increased ease, speed, and confidence.

Visual impairment is not solely related to issues with the eyes; it can arise from various factors. Common causes include challenges with eyesight, the development of cataracts, problems in the rear part of the eye, or issues with the optic nerve. Individuals experiencing visual impairment often encounter numerous challenges in their daily lives. Limited vision can result in difficulties in safely crossing roads, determining when to proceed at traffic lights, or navigating around obstacles. For educational pursuits, those with visual impairment typically need to learn braille, a tactile system of raised dots. Even routine tasks, such as visiting a doctor, become challenging as they rely on touch and hearing to navigate their surroundings.

In today's context, AI is a prominent and rapidly advancing field. Focused on imbuing systems with intelligence, AI is anticipated to play a substantial role in the future [5]. The progression of AI is driven by the abundance of data and powerful computing resources. Its applications extend across various domains, including retail and delivery services. In the retail sector, AI facilitates inventory management by tracking stock levels and identifying popular items, thereby enhancing the overall shopping experience. Moreover, AI can deliver personalized product recommendations based on individual preferences. In the realm of delivery services, AI excels in planning the most efficient routes for transporting items from one location to another.

AI is significantly reshaping the landscape of education by providing valuable tools for teachers to gauge students' progress and offer tailored support based on individual needs. This personalized approach enhances the learning experience for each student. In the realm of security, AI stands as a powerful ally for law enforcement. Its advanced image processing capabilities enable the identification of suspects in surveillance videos, contributing to more effective and efficient investigations. Moreover, AI plays a crucial role in the surveillance of buildings, promptly alerting authorities to any unusual activities and bolstering overall security measures.

Incorporating AI into ophthalmological practices revolutionizes the landscape of eye care, offering unprecedented opportunities for early intervention and precision medicine. The seamless integration of AI algorithms with contact fundus examination devices streamlines the diagnostic process, empowering clinicians to deliver timely and tailored interventions. Moreover, AI-driven analysis of fundus images enables the detection of subtle abnormalities that may escape human observation, ensuring comprehensive assessment and treatment planning. As the field continues to evolve, the collaborative efforts between AI technology and medical expertise promise to redefine standards of care and elevate the quality of life for patients with ocular conditions.

LITERATURE REVIEW

Cheng and colleagues have put forth a holistic strategy to improve visual localization for individuals with visual impairments [6]. Their established system comprises diverse components, such as a profound descriptor network, validation of 2D–3D geometry, and real-time sequence matching. The process involves the integration of a dual descriptor network with RGB, infrared, and depth images, allowing for the creation of resilient, contextually rich descriptors and local features.

Researchers associated with Springer-Verilog have introduced an innovative LiDAR-based technique designed for the effective prediction of distances and precise measurement of obstacles [7]. Their approach involves employing a lightweight deep learning model, specifically customized for obstacle detection, known as EfficientDet-LiteV4. The anticipated distances are calculated by analyzing depth maps generated by LiDAR. To assess the efficacy of their approach, they implemented it on a Raspberry Pi4 platform integrated with Li- DAR. The experimental results reveal a notable level of accuracy in both obstacle detection and distance estimation.

Shen and collaborators have introduced a novel two-stage aggregate learning model named VRTMM, designed for the interpretation of remote sensing images [8]. This model seamlessly combines a variational autoencoder with reinforcement learning. In the initial stage, a convolutional neural network (CNN) undergoes fine-tuning with the assistance of a variational autoencoder. Following this, in the second stage, a transformer-based model is employed to generate text descriptions, utilizing both spatial and semantic features.

A tool is designed that utilizes optical character recognition (OCR) to scan text from images and translate it into spoken language. The method, detailed in a study [9], employs a specific algorithm called MSER to ensure accuracy. The setup involves connecting a Raspberry Pi to a camera for capturing images, and the MSER algorithm is utilized to extract words. On average, this system achieves an accuracy rate of about 85-87%.

An advanced audio assistance system is crafted to assist visually impaired individuals by reading text from documents and identifying nearby objects, as outlined in [10]. This technology detects both text in documents and obstacles, providing voice-based audio output to aid visually impaired individuals in navigating their surroundings effectively. Another tool, designed for individuals with visual challenges [11], utilizes smart technology and Raspberry Pi 2, programmed with Python, to help them read books. Equipped with a monitor to track program performance, it is particularly useful for blind students, albeit currently supporting only one language.

A beneficial system is developed for individuals with partial vision using a Raspberry Pi 3 and OCR technology, as described in [12]. This system assists those with limited vision by converting text from documents into spoken words. However, one limitation is the increased complexity as a computer is required for image processing.

This study presents a fresh method to locate visually impaired individuals indoors by leveraging smartphone sensors. Utilizing sensor data determines the person's position within a confined space. Evaluations using movement patterns resembling those of visually impaired individuals displayed promising results, achieving an accuracy close to the length of a typical step (around 0.65 meters) within a 0.6 by 0.6-meter area. Subsequent investigations will explore the impact of different area sizes and magnetic fluctuations on accuracy. Notably, this method currently relies on an internet connection, prompting researchers to seek ways to enable its operation offline. Additionally, they aim to enhance indoor navigation for the visually impaired.

TECHNOLOGIES

In this study, deep learning serves as the overarching framework, enabling the exploration and application of advanced computer vision techniques. YOLO and RCNN represent two distinct methodologies within deep learning, each offering unique insights into object detection and recognition. By leveraging these foundational approaches, the study aims to delve into the intricacies of image analysis and enhance our understanding of complex visual data.

Deep Learning

Deep learning, a computer program mirroring the brain's neural network, operates through deep neural networks, a subset of machine learning [12]. These networks consist of connected layers, starting with the input layer on top and the output layer at the bottom, while the in-between layers are termed hidden layers. The “deep” aspect refers to networks connecting neurons across multiple layers. Each Hidden layer contains neurons with interconnections between them, processing input signals through activation functions, weights, and biases. Deep learning networks process extensive input data through multiple layers, facilitating the learning of intricate data features. In contrast, shallow neural networks contain only one hidden layer, while deep learning networks encompass numerous layers, as seen in models like Google LeNet's 22 layers. Presently, deep learning finds applications in various domains, including TV, autonomous vehicles, smartphones, and fraud detection, among others.

The most basic artificial neural network is a computational model that allows information to move in one direction. In this architecture, information begins at the input layer, progresses through the intermediate “hidden” layers, and eventually culminates at the output layer without any feedback loops within the network. The output layer is where the flow of information concludes.

YOLO Algorithm

YOLO, an acronym for “You Only Look Once”, represents a real-time technique utilized for identifying multiple objects within images Fig. (1). YOLO is an object detection system that redefines object detection as a single regression task, mapping image pixels directly to bounding box coordinates and class probabilities.

Fig. (1))
YOLO architecture.

YOLO is an object detection system that swiftly analyzes images through a single pass via a convolutional neural network. It directly forecasts bounding boxes and class probabilities, facilitating rapid and precise object detection in real time.

The typical YOLO architecture consists of 24 convolutional layers followed by 2 fully connected layers. It divides the image into an SxS grid and predicts bounding boxes and class probabilities for each grid cell [13]. The loss function in YOLO accounts for errors in classification, localization, and confidence. YOLO is trained on the COCO dataset and employs techniques such as batch normalization, dropout, and multi-scale training to improve performance.

This method employs convolutional neural networks (CNN) to swiftly detect entities, accomplishing object detection with a single forward propagation through the network. YOLO's approach suggests that a sole algorithm run is adequate for object detection across the entire image, predicting multiple class probabilities and bounding boxes concurrently. Notably, the YOLO algorithm encompasses various versions, including the well-known Tiny YOLO and YOLOv5.

The YOLO algorithm holds significance due to several crucial aspects:

A. Rapid Detection: YOLO substantially accelerates object detection by efficiently forecasting objects in real time.

B. Precision: Its forecasting technique yields highly accurate results, minimizing background errors.

C. Learning Proficiency: With advanced learning capabilities, YOLO distinguishes object representations effectively, aiding in the detection of diverse objects.

The YOLO algorithm operates through the implementation of three specific strategies:

• Residual blocks

• Bounding box regression

• Intersection Over Union (IOU)

Residual Blocks

Initially, the image is partitioned into multiple grid sections, each measuring SxS. A visual representation illustrates the division of the input image into uniformly sized grids. These grid cells operate autonomously, focusing on detecting objects within their assigned boundaries. If the center of an object resides within a particular grid cell, that cell assumes responsibility for recognizing the object.

Bounding Box Regression

A “bounding box” delineates an object within an image. Each bounding box within the image encompasses:

• Width (bw)

• Height (bh)

• Object type (e.g., person, car, traffic signal, etc.) represented as “c”. The center coordinates of the bounding box are given as (bx, by).

The YOLO method employs a distinctive bounding box regression technique to anticipate the size, placement, and classification of objects. The visual representation presented illustrates the probability of an object existing within the bounding box.

Intersection Over Union (IOU) measures the overlap between bounding boxes in object identification. YOLO employs IOU to accurately define the output bounding box encompassing the identified objects. Each grid cell predicts bounding boxes and their confidence levels. After extracting region proposals, labeling is essential for training. An IOU of 1 denotes proposals with at least 0.5 IOU values with bounding boxes, and their respective classes are accepted. How-

ever, proposals below 0.3 IOU are deemed background, discarding the rest for consideration.

Region-based Convolutional Neural Network (RCNN)

Upon CNN's coverage, computer vision surged in popularity, especially with the forefront presence of self-driving cars [14]. Object detection is integral within computer vision, aiding in tasks like pose estimation, surveillance, and more. Object detection algorithms differ from classification algorithms by creating bounding boxes around identified objects within images [15].

In this scenario, multiple bounding boxes might represent diverse aspects of interest, and their quantity can vary. This variability in the output layer's length, due to uncertain object rates, prevents solving this by adding a fully connected layer atop a typical convolutional network. Addressing these challenges involves isolating specific regions for interpretation by a CNN algorithm to determine object presence (Fig. 2). Prior approaches demanded numerous regions, posing computational risks. R-CNN, YOLO, and similar algorithms emerged swiftly to address these issues.

Fig. (2))
RCNN region with CNN Feature.

RCNN employs a two-stage architecture to detect objects in images. It first proposes regions of interest using selective search and then extracts CNN features from these regions for classification.

PROPOSED METHOD

The COCO dataset (Fig. 3), an acronym for Common Objects in Context, was created to enhance image comprehension [16]. This dataset is commonly utilized in computer vision due to its high-quality visual data, frequently employed to evaluate algorithms and assess real-time object detection capabilities. Leading neural network libraries readily interpret COCO dataset formats.

Fig. (3))
COCO Data Set.

Key Features of COCO:

• Detailed object segmentation with prototype annotations

• Contextual understanding

• Superpixel stuff segmentation

• Over 200,000 labeled images out of the total 330,000

• 1.5 million object instances

• Comprises 80 object categories, referred to as “COCO classes,” encompassing various easily identifiable entities (humans, vehicles, furniture, etc.) for precise labeling purposes.

The COCO dataset is a widely used collection of images annotated with object labels and segmentation masks. It serves as a benchmark for various computer vision tasks such as object detection, segmentation, and captioning.

The system utilizes the COCO dataset for training and evaluation, with preprocessing steps that include data augmentation techniques like flipping, rotation, and scaling to increase training data diversity, normalization to achieve zero mean and unit variance for faster convergence, and conversion of COCO annotations to the YOLO network format. The model is trained using a combination of cross-entropy loss for classification and mean squared error for bounding box regression, employing batch training with mini-batches to balance memory usage and training speed and a dynamic learning rate schedule for steady convergence. Performance is validated on a separate validation set to tune hyperparameters and avoid overfitting.

In the domain of visual comprehension, the proposed system initiates the process by loading images or videos. Subsequently, it employs Object Detection to identify various objects and Object Tracking to monitor their movements. The computational core, a Deep Neural Network (DNN), learns from both visual input and tracking data, as shown in the block diagram Fig. (4). This DNN then processes the information, translating it into straightforward descriptions of the recognized objects. Finally, a Text-to-Speech feature transforms these descriptions into spoken words, providing an accessible means to comprehend the content of the images. This cohesive procedure, integrating detection, tracking, and deep learning, presents exciting prospects for rendering visual information into a language format.

Fig. (4))
Block diagram of proposed work.

A system for visual understanding begins by inputting images or videos and then uses object detection and tracking to identify and monitor objects. A deep neural network learns from this data, generating descriptions, and a text-to-speech feature vocalizes them, enabling accessible comprehension of visual content.

In objective detection, finding objects in a picture usually starts by cleaning up the image (removing unnecessary stuff). After that, we look for important features like lines and regions, even focusing on specific textures. Understanding and spotting things in a picture or video is possible with a cool technology called object detection. We use something called RCNN to draw boxes around the things we find. In Object Tracking, imagine using a camera and a special detection method to follow a moving object over time. This method, called blob detection, looks for parts in a digital image that stand out, like being brighter or a different color. These standout parts are called blobs, and in terms of recognizing objects, they can show where things are in the picture. People often use YOLO with RCNN for this. YOLO quickly predicts things in the entire image in just one go.

Think of a DNN as the digital equivalent of the neural network in the human brain. It is a subset of machine learning known as deep learning. Deep learning employs algorithms organized into layers, consisting of an Input Layer where data originates, an Output Layer representing the endpoint, and Hidden Layers positioned in the intermediate stages. “Deep” means the network has more than two levels of connections. Each Hidden layer activates a neuron. The network handles lots of data, learning complex things about the data as it goes through each layer. While our initial experiments used a subset of the COCO dataset with over 50 images for preliminary tests, the final training utilized the full COCO dataset. This extensive dataset ensures that the model learns a wide variety of object appearances and contexts, leading to more accurate and robust performance. The computer can now tell what's in a photo, like animals, a keyboard, a mouse, and other things. We used a pre-trained model called COCO (common object in context). With a tool called OpenCV-contrib-python, the computer compares what it sees to its trained database. Then, it labels the pictures, turning the image into words. After that, the computer says what it sees using a winsound tool and speaks the words with a package called pyttsx3.

Software Packages Utilised

Utilised software packages and technologies are:

  • NumPy: Utilized for efficient numerical computations, especially for array manipulations required during data preprocessing and augmentation.
  • OpenCV: Employed for image processing tasks such as reading, resizing, and displaying images and videos. It also assists in the implementation of computer vision algorithms.
  • pyttsx3: This library is used for converting text labels into speech, providing an offline solution for text-to-speech conversion.
  • PyWin32: Allows integration with Windows-specific features, such as accessing system-specific functionalities required by the text-to-speech engine.
  • OpenCV-contrib-python: Adds extra functionalities to OpenCV, including advanced image processing algorithms and utilities for object tracking.
  • winsound: Used to control the system's sound output, ensuring that the detected objects' labels are audibly communicated to the user.

RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

The 'coco' database, an online repository of pre-trained objects, serves as the default option with the added flexibility to choose an alternative database. In the execution of the code, the OpenCV package activates the camera module systematically, identifying and labeling objects within its visual field. The camera consistently and concurrently labels 5-6 objects, clearly depicted in Figs. (5 and 6).

Fig. (5))
Detection of person by camera module.

The camera concurrently identifies 2 persons and 2 objects using a deep neural network (DNN) that learns from visual input. Subsequently, the DNN processes the data, converting it into clear descriptions of the identified objects.

This procedural sequence provides a comprehensive understanding of the essential steps in executing the Python program. Commencing with the importation of critical libraries such as cv2, imutils, numpy, and pyttsx3, the process involves defining a text-to-speech (TTS) function, loading the YOLO model along with class names, and opening a camera feed. Subsequently, a loop is initiated for continuous frame processing.

Fig. (6))
Detection of an object by the camera module.

The camera simultaneously recognizes one individual and a book through DNN.

Within this loop, frames undergo systematic resizing, objects are detected using YOLO, and the identified objects are both visually displayed and audibly announced using TTS. This iterative process continues until user termination by pressing 'q,' leading to the release of the video capture. Following object detection, the winsound package is employed to audibly articulate the identified objects, leveraging Windows' sound-playing capabilities.

To boost functionality, users can choose to record identified objects into a distinct file, as demonstrated in windows. Efficient program operation hinges on utilizing an appropriate Python development environment or IDE.

For enhanced functionality, users have the option to log detected objects into a separate file, a feature illustrated in Windows Fig. (7). Effective program execution relies on the use of a suitable Python development environment or IDE.

Quantitative Metrics

The performance of the system is evaluated using several metrics:

  • Mean Average Precision (mAP): This metric measures the accuracy of the object detection system, considering both precision and recall.
  • Intersection over Union (IoU): This metric evaluates the overlap between the predicted bounding boxes and the ground truth boxes.
  • Frames per Second (FPS): This metric measures the real-time detection speed of the system.
Fig. (7))
List of detected objects.
Table 1 Experimental results.
Model mAP IoU FPS
YOLOv3 57.9 70.3 45
Tiny YOLO 34.6 50.8 220
RCNN 66.0 75.2 2

The proposed system, using YOLOv3, demonstrates a good balance between accuracy and speed, making it suitable for real-time applications. Tiny YOLO, while faster, sacrifices some accuracy, whereas RCNN offers higher accuracy but at the cost of real-time performance (Table 1).

Discussion on Limitations

  • Accuracy: While YOLOv3 offers a good balance, there is room for improvement in precision and recall.
  • Real-time Constraints: Although YOLOv3 performs well in real-time, certain applications may require even faster detection speeds.
  • Environment Dependence: The system's performance may vary in different environmental conditions, such as lighting and background complexity.
  • Future Directions and Potential Extensions: Improving accuracy can be achieved through advanced architectures like YOLOv4, EfficientDet, or attention mechanisms. Dataset expansion with diverse data enhances model robustness. Implementing models on edge devices reduces latency and enhances privacy. Integrating visual data with sensory inputs like audio or haptic feedback improves user experience. Potential extensions involve indoor navigation for visually impaired users, interactive object querying, multi-language support, and integration with autonomous vehicles for navigation.

CONCLUSION

In conclusion, YOLO stands out as a rapid and versatile method for efficiently detecting a variety of objects in diverse scenarios. Its capability to convert images into text and spoken words is particularly beneficial for individuals with visual impairments, aiding them in comprehending visual content. This study underscores YOLO's swift object recognition and its crucial role in enhancing accessibility, especially for those facing vision challenges. By combining various technologies, the research aims to expedite the process of object identification, ultimately improving the overall understanding of visual content. Essentially, YOLO emerges as a pivotal player in this effort, showcasing its effectiveness beyond traditional methods and making a substantial contribution to a more inclusive approach to interpreting the visual world.

AI-Assisted Crop Management Using the LSTM Model in Smart Farming



Prabakaran Natarajan1, *, Abhijai Rajawat1, Akshat Chaube1, Anshul Mahlavat1, Ramanathan Lakshmanan1
1 School of Computer Science and Engineering, Vellore Institute of Technology, Vellore 632014, India

Abstract

The use of information and communication technologies in agriculture to increase productivity, efficiency, and sustainability is known as smart farming. The implementation of predictive analytics and data-driven insights in smart farming enhances the effectiveness of agricultural systems as a whole, decision-making processes, and resource allocation. This study compares the use of the algorithms long short-term memory (LSTM), support vector machine (SVM), deep belief network, Naive Bayes, artificial neural network (ANN), and gated recurrent unit (GRU) in optimizing agricultural operations. The study highlights how using LSTM in smart farming has the ability to transform traditional agricultural methods, resulting in sustainable, higher-yield output while minimizing resource loss and environmental impact. Smart farming has emerged as a new approach for modernizing and optimizing agricultural practices through the integration of cutting-edge technology, with a focus on machine learning in particular. Various algorithms were applied to this dataset, producing measurable results like accuracy, loss, correct detection rate (CDR), and false discovery rate (FDR). Through an extensive comparative study, it was identified that the long short-term memory (LSTM) algorithm was the most promising choice for the dataset. Following the application of machine learning algorithms on different training to test splits like 80-20, 70-30, 65-35, 60-40, and 55-45, it was found that LSTM has the best accuracy on average, ranging from 95% to 98%. The exploration of LSTM demonstrated its potential to significantly enhance decision-making processes for farmers and researchers, ultimately improving agricultural efficiency and outcomes.

Keywords: Artificial intelligence, Artificial neural network, Correct detection rate, Deep belief network, False discovery rate, Gated recurrent unit, Hyperparameter tuning, Logistic regression, Long short-term memory, Machine learning algorithms, Model architecture enhancements, Naive bayes, Posterior probability, Precision agriculture, Random forest, Rectified linear unit, Smart farming, Support vector machine, Sustainability, Yield optimization.

INTRODUCTION

When it comes to precision agriculture, where every decision and piece of information are highly relevant, the LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory) algorithm is the cornerstone of innovation. Modern technology has sparked a revolution in agriculture, transforming conventional farming methods into ones that are more effective and sustainable. Intelligent crop management, a multifaceted strategy that seamlessly combines cutting-edge technology [1], most notably machine learning (ML) and the strength of LSTM algorithms [2], is the foundation of smart farming. The key to this agricultural transformation is to carefully optimize procedures and maximize harvests while using as little of the planet's resources as possible. Farmers now have access to the priceless tool of precision agriculture thanks to ML, particularly in the context of that practice.

Farmers now have the power to precisely apply fertilizers, pesticides, and other inputs in strict conformity with regional requirements thanks to LSTM algorithms. These algorithms examine the minute details of soil properties and crop features to produce individualized recommendations that promote the wise use of resources while simultaneously reducing the damaging environmental impact. This well-planned strategy, made possible by LSTM-driven ML, provides a double benefit. On the one hand, prices are successfully lowered, and on the other, sustainability takes center stage with a much lower chance of soil damage. In addition, LSTM algorithms are skilled at keeping track of soil moisture levels, incorporating weather forecasts, and tracking the changing requirements of plants to optimize watering schedules. The conventional distinction between resource conservation and agricultural output is effectively blurred by this finely tuned precision, which not only preserves limited water supplies but also ushers in increased crop yields [3]. LSTM algorithms go even further in the constantly changing world of agriculture, where data-driven insights are the key to success. They are essential for accurate yield estimation and careful crop monitoring. These algorithms examine data from a variety of sources, including satellite imaging and sensor data, and have the ability to analyze crop growth patterns, identify nutrient deficiencies, and compute crop yields [4]. With the abundance of knowledge they now possess, farmers are better able to plan their markets and identify the best time to harvest their crops, thus increasing profitability and ushering in a new era for agriculture.

Factors

Phosphorus Level of Soil

A crucial macronutrient for plants, phosphorus is necessary for several physiological activities. Integrating soil phosphorus levels into crop prediction models leads to a more comprehensive understanding of nutrient dynamics [5] and facilitates informed decisions regarding fertilization strategies, soil management practices, and overall nutrient optimization.

Potassium Level of Soil

An essential component for the growth and development of plants is potassium. It is engaged in several crucial plant processes, including the movement of nutrients and water throughout the plant, control over the growth and development of the plant, energy storage, and resistance to disease and pests [6].

Temperature

One of the most significant variables influencing crop growth is temperature. Farmers can calculate how much water and fertilizer they will require by knowing the typical temperature for the growing season. They may schedule the planting and harvesting of crops using temperature data as well [7].

Humidity

Humidity, defined as the amount of moisture present in the air, influences several critical physiological processes in plants, including transpiration, stomatal conductance, and photosynthesis. By considering humidity as a key variable in crop prediction models, deeper insights into the moisture stress experienced by crops can be gained, enabling more accurate predictions about their growth, yield potential, and overall health [8].

PH of Soil

The pH of the soil indicates how acidic or alkaline it is. Various soil pH values are preferred by different crops. Plants may find it difficult to absorb nutrients if the pH of the soil is too high or too low, which could result in stunted growth, low yields, or even death.

Rainfall

Most of the previous irrigation systems did not take weather forecasting information into account when choosing irrigation strategies [9]. When rain is promptly followed by crop irrigation, it wastes freshwater and energy and stunts crop growth. By utilizing weather forecasting data, ML-based solutions can handle these situations and improve irrigation decision support.

Soil Moisture

The amount of water in the soil is referred to as its moisture content. Numerous variables, such as the kind of soil, the climate, and the quantity of rainfall, can affect how moist the soil is. Gaining more knowledge on the relationship between soil moisture and crop growth can help farmers make more informed decisions about when, where, and how much water to use when planting crops. This could lead to higher yields and increased profitability [10].

In smart farming, the integration of cutting-edge technologies and data-driven strategies optimizes agricultural practices, considering factors such as pH levels, humidity, potassium (K), rainfall, and temperature, which can be noted in Fig. (1) for efficient and sustainable farming.

Fig. (1))
Elements of Smart Farming.

Approaches

Long Short Term Memory (LSTM)

With multiple customized variants, LSTM, a specialized recurrent neural network (RNN) architecture, excels in sequential data processing. Precise control over information flow is possible thanks to the forget, output, and input gates of the vanilla LSTM [2]. For a thorough knowledge of the context, the bidirectional LSTM processes input in both directions using the equations from (1-6). Through the provision of data-driven insights, it makes use of past environmental data to forecast disease and insect outbreaks, estimate agricultural yields, improve resource management, and predict crop growth stages [11, 28].

Candidate Hidden State (Ct):

Cell State (Ct) Update:

Computational Complexity for LSTM

Training Time Complexity: O(k . n . m^2)

- k is the number of training epochs

- n is the number of training samples

- m is the number of LSTM units

Inference Time Complexity: O(m^2)

Scalability and Efficiency

Because LSTMs can handle sequential data, they are a good fit for time-series forecasting in smart farming, which includes agricultural production forecasting and weather prediction. However, when dealing with lengthy sequences and huge datasets, their high training complexity may become a bottleneck. As a result, they are less scalable for real-time applications requiring powerful computers.

Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU)

It is a specific type of recurrent neural network (RNN) that works well for sequence modeling applications. GRUs are excellent for jobs like estimating crop yields, forecasting weather patterns, and spotting pests and diseases because they can learn long-term connections in sequential data. GRUs can be used to create a range of predictive models for smart farming. For instance, a GRU might be used to forecast a crop's yield given past data and the present weather. GRUs can be utilized to create disease and pest early warning systems [12]. These gates are expressed using equations (7-10).

Update Gate (zt):

Reset Gate (rt):

(8)

Candidate Hidden State (h't):

(9)

Hidden State (ht) Update:

Computational Complexity

- Raining Time Complexity: O(k . n . m^2)

- Like LSTM but generally more efficient

- Inference Time Complexity: O(m^2)

Scalability and Efficiency

Despite being like LSTMs, GRUs have a simpler architecture, which frequently leads to quicker timings for inference and training.

Support Vector Machine (SVM)

Regression and classification problems can be handled by the machine learning method named support vector machines (SVMs). Tasks with a large number of characteristics and a small number of training samples are especially well-suited for SVMs [13]. This is frequently the case in smart farming, where thousands of features may be present but only a few hundred training samples. Based on variables including crop type, weather, and soil quality, SVMs can be used to forecast agricultural yield [14]. Farmers can utilize this information to make more informed decisions on fertilization, irrigation, and planting [15].

(11)

In our model (11), 'f(x)' is the decision function for input 'x,' while 'i' represents Lagrange multipliers. 'yi' denotes class labels for training samples, and 'K(x, xi)' computes similarity. 'b' is the bias term. These components collectively shape our classification model, optimizing its accuracy and decision boundary.

Computational Complexity

Training Time Complexity:

- Linear SVM: O(n . d)

- Non-linear SVM (using kernel methods): Between O(n^2 . d) and O(n^3 . d)

- n is the number of training samples

- d is the number of features

Inference Time Complexity: O(n . d) for linear, O(s . d) for non-linear (where s is the number of support vectors)

Scalability and Efficiency

SVMs are useful for tasks like crop classification from spectral data because they can handle vast feature spaces and are computationally cheap, especially for linear models. Though less scalable and more computationally demanding, non-linear support vector machines (SVMs) provide superior accuracy for intricate patterns. The size of the dataset and the complexity of the kernel being utilized determine how effective they are in smart farming.

Naive Bayes

A straightforward yet effective machine learning approach for classification tasks is naive Bayes. It is founded on the Bayes theorem, which asserts that the likelihood of an event occurring relies on the likelihood of the event occurring under specific circumstances [14]. Naive Bayes can be used in the context of smart farming to categorize crops, pests, and illnesses based on a variety of characteristics, including soil characteristics, meteorological information, and sensor readings [16].

(12)

Equation (12) has the posterior probability, P(y | X), which represents the probability of a particular class y given a set of features X. The likelihood, P(X | y), reflects the probability of observing these features X given class y. The prior probability, P(y), denotes the initial probability of class y, while the marginal likelihood, P(X), represents the overall likelihood of the observed features X.

Computational Complexity

Training Time Complexity: O(n.d)

- n is the number of training samples

- d is the number of features

Inference Time Complexity: O(d)

Scalability and Efficiency

Naive Bayes’ straightforward probabilistic methodology makes it extremely effective and scalable. It works well for smart farming applications that require prompt and comprehensible conclusions, including crop disease prediction using basic feature sets. Its ability to handle big datasets efficiently is guaranteed by its linear complexity.

Artificial Neural Network (ANN)

One area of machine learning that draws inspiration from the human brain is known as an “Artificial Neural Network” (ANN) [9]. It is a kind of computational network whose architecture is intended to resemble the biological neural networks present in the brain. ANN is used in the context of pH and moisture sensors to gather and process data from these sensors [17]. The ANN can forecast crop yields in certain fields since the data values are tested and trained within the system (13).

(13)

The output, denoted as f(y), is determined by a weighted sum of its input values (yi), each multiplied by corresponding weights (wi). Additionally, a bias term (c) is added to this weighted sum. The result then passes through an activation function (typically sigmoid, ReLU, or similar) to introduce non-linearity, shaping the neuron's final output.

Computational Complexity

Training Time Complexity: O(k.n.m)

- k is the number of training epochs

- n is the number of training samples

- m is the number of neurons per layer

Inference Time Complexity: O(m)

Scalability and Efficiency

Because of their adaptability, ANNs can be used for a wide range of smart farming applications, including yield prediction and disease detection. Depending on the size and depth of the network, their complexity is less than that of deep networks like DBNs, which makes them more scalable and efficient for real-time deployment.

Deep Belief Network

A type of artificial neural network called a Deep Belief Network (DBN) is made up of many layers of hidden units. DBNs are advantageous for a range of applications, including image classification, object identification, and natural language processing, because they have the capacity to learn complicated patterns in data [16].

Based on a range of variables, including soil characteristics, meteorological information, and previous yields, DBNs can be used to forecast crop yields. Informed choices concerning irrigation, fertilization, and planting can be made using this knowledge.

(14)

This model (14) has two layers: the visible layer (v) and the hidden layer (h). The connections between them are represented by weights (Wij), while each unit in the visible layer has a bias (ai), and each unit in the hidden layer has a bias (bj).

Computational Complexity

Training Time Complexity: O(l . n . m^2)

- l is the number of layers

- n is the number of training samples

- m is the number of hidden units per layer

Inference Time Complexity: O(m^2)

Scalability and Efficiency

Because of their deep designs and layer-wise pre-training, DBNs require a lot of processing power. They are less scalable for real-time smart farming applications even though they are capable of learning complicated representations because of their prohibitively long training times. They work better in offline analytical tasks when there is less of an emphasis on computing performance.

The research initiative was dedicated to the extensive exploration of crop management within the context of smart farming. To achieve this, a diverse array of modern deep learning and machine learning algorithms were used. The methodological foundation is meticulously outlined in Figs. (1 and 2) serves as a roadmap, defining the crucial factors and approaches central to the study.
